To improve it and get to O(n), we have to remove redundant work, and that\'s where the sliding window technique comes in.\n\nConsider the sub-array starting at position i+1 and ending at j (a[i+1:j]). If we add a[i] to the sub-array, we can convert it to a[i:j+1] using O(1) time. This means once we have the sum in a[i+1:j], we can easily get the sum in a[i:j+1] without needing to add up all elements again. All we need to do is subtract a[i].\n\nAlgorithm/Pseudocode:\n1. Initialize an "answer pair" where the answer will be the sub-array from start to end (inclusive).\n2. Initialize a variable sum holding the sum of the current sub-array a[start:end]. Ensure it is initialized to 0.\n3. Begin looping from start = 0 and end = 0.\n4. While we are able to enlarge the sub-array (i.e., we have not crossed the array boundary/iterated through the entire array from end = 0 to i = len(a)-1 inclusively), do the following:\n    a. Increment sum by a[end].\n    b. If this sum is greater than k, then increment start. Note that this operation effectively removes elements from the left side of the sub-array to get it to sum to less or equal to k. It provides a fully managed platform that supports multiple programming languages, including .NET, Java, Node.js, Python, and PHP.\n\nWhen deploying an application to Azure App Services, there are three main concepts to consider: resource groups, plans, and web applications.\n\n1. Resource Group: A resource group is a logical container in Azure that holds related resources, such as virtual machines, storage accounts, databases, and web applications. Resource groups simplify resource management by enabling you to group related resources together, allowing you to manage them as a single entity. For example, you can deploy, update, and delete all the resources in a resource group simultaneously. A resource group also provides a way to manage access control and permissions for a set of resources, allowing you to restrict access to specific resources or groups of resources.\n2. Plan: A plan defines the infrastructure and resources available to an application, such as the number of CPU cores, memory, and storage capacity. Azure App Services offers several types of plans, including Free, Shared, Basic, Standard, Premium, and Isolated plans. Each plan offers different features and capabilities, such as automatic scaling, custom domains, and SSL support, and the plan selected determines the cost of running the application in Azure.\n3. Web Application: A web application is a specific instance of an application deployed to Azure App Services. Each web application is associated with a specific plan and resource group, and the web application can be accessed via a unique URL. Multiple web applications can be deployed to the same resource group and plan, and each web application can be managed independently, with its own settings and configurations.\n\nThe relationship between resource groups, plans, and web applications in Azure App Services is hierarchical. Resource groups contain plans, and plans contain web applications. Web applications inherit the settings and configurations of their associated plans, and plans determine the resources available to web applications. The goal is to allow websites to consistently support features that work across various browsers by providing a shim for functionality that isn\'t built-in.\n\nThe term "polyfill" was coined by Remy Sharp and comes from a British usage of the word "polyfiller" (a paste used to fill holes in plaster walls). Essentially, it\'s a method of simulating modern functionality on older browsers that do not natively support it.\n\nPolyfills are important in modern development for a few key reasons:\n\n1. In a client-server architecture, clients request resources from a central server, while in a peer-to-peer architecture, each node is both a client and a server. This means that each node can request resources from any other node. The main difference between the two is the centralization of resources. In client-server architecture, resources are centralized and controlled by the server, while in peer-to-peer architecture, resources are distributed across all nodes. Both architectures have their advantages and disadvantages. Client-server architecture is more secure and scalable, while peer-to-peer architecture is more decentralized and flexible.\n2. Cellular networks and WiFi networks are two popular wireless communication technologies. Cellular networks have a larger range and are designed for mobile devices, while WiFi networks have a smaller range and are designed for stationary devices. Cellular networks have lower bandwidth, while WiFi networks have higher bandwidth. Cellular networks are more expensive to use, while WiFi networks are usually free to use. Cellular networks are better suited for applications that require mobility and coverage over a large area, while WiFi networks are better suited for applications that require higher bandwidth and lower cost.\n3. A content provider network is a network that enables content delivery to end-users from a central location. A content provider network typically includes a set of servers and caching nodes that store and deliver content to end-users. This network is designed to improve the performance and reliability of content delivery. Content provider networks are commonly used for delivering video content, software updates, and other large files.\n4. Local Area Networks (LANs), Metropolitan Area Networks (MANs), and Wide Area Networks (WANs) are three common types of networks. LANs are typically limited to a single building or campus, while MANs cover a larger area, typically a city or metropolitan area. WANs cover even larger areas, such as a region or country. LANs typically have a high speed and low cost, while MANs have a lower speed and higher cost. WANs have the lowest speed and highest cost. LANs are typically used for connecting computers within a single organization, while MANs are used for connecting computers within a larger area. WANs are used for connecting computers across different regions or countries.\n5. The TCP/IP and OSI networking models are two popular models used for interoperability and communication in networks. Both models have a layered architecture, but they differ in the number of layers and the functions of each layer. TCP/IP has 4 layers, while OSI has 7 layers. The TCP/IP model includes application, transport, internet, and link layers. The OSI model includes physical, data link, network, transport, session, presentation, and application layers. TCP/IP combines several OSI layers for efficiency, while OSI is more detailed and comprehensive. Some examples of protocols associated with each model include HTTP (application layer of TCP/IP), TCP (transport layer of TCP/IP), IP (internet layer of TCP/IP), and Ethernet (link layer of TCP/IP). For OSI, examples include ATM (physical layer), SDLC (data link layer), IP (network layer), TCP (transport layer), RPC (session layer), X.200 (presentation layer), and FTP (application layer).\n6. Guided and unguided communication mediums are two types of transmission media used for data transfer. Guided communication media include wires, fiber optic cables, and coaxial cables. Unguided communication media include radio waves, microwaves, and infrared signals. The other pointer, `end`, initialized to 0, represents the index where next element will be added to the subarray.\n\nWe continuously increase `end` and sum elements starting from `start` to `end` while maintaining sum not greater than `k`. If sum reaches `k`, we refresh the sum by subtracting the element at the last index of the previous sum, effectively moving `start` to next index.\n\nSteps:\n1. Initialize two pointers `start` and `end` to 0, and variable `sum` to `a[0]`. `start` represents start of subarray and `end` represents end of subarray. `sum` keeps track of sum of elements in current subarray.\n2. Continue from step 3, while `end` is less than `length` of array `a`.\n3. If sum reaches `k`, move `start` to next index and subtract its corresponding `a[start-1]` from `sum`.\n4. Add `a[end]` to `sum`.\n5. Update `end` to `end + 1`.\n6. Compare length of current subarray with maximum length found so far and update if current one is larger.\n7. The problem is divided into sub-problems and solved concurrently. This approach often leads to a reduction in completion time.\n\n### Why Parallel Computing?\n\n1. **Speed**: Parallel computing allows tasks to be accomplished in less time by breaking them down into smaller tasks and processing them simultaneously.\n\n2. **Efficiency**: It allows for the efficient use of resources by maximizing the use of available processing power.\n\n3. **Complexity**: Some problems inherently require parallel computation due to their complexity or large data sets.\n\n### Goals of Parallel Computing\n\n1. **Increase in Speed**: The primary goal is to reduce the time taken to complete a task compared to a sequential computing approach.\n\n2. **Increase in Throughput**: Parallel computing aims to increase the total amount of work that can be done in a unit of time.\n\n3. **Increase in Scalability**: It aims to accommodate larger problems than could be handled by a single computer.\n\n### Applications\n\n1. **Scientific Computing**: Used in fields like physics, biology, and chemistry for complex simulations and mathematical computations.\n\n2. **Machine Learning**: It's used for training machine learning models, where large amounts of data need to be processed.\n\n3. **Data Mining**: Used in data mining to process and analyze big data more efficiently.\n\n4. **Computer Graphics**: It's used in 3D graphics rendering and processing.\n\n5. **Cryptography**: Parallel computing is used in the encryption and decryption of codes.\n\n### Advantages\n\n1. **Speed**: Parallel computing enables tasks to be completed faster by dividing them among multiple processors.\n\n2. **Scalability**: Adding more processing units allows for handling larger problems.\n\n3. **Economy**: By reducing the time taken to solve problems, the overall cost
